<a name="section-13"></a>
## 27-32: Non-Carious Lesions, MID, Colour, Bleaching, Lasers

### 1. Vital Bleaching - 4m (483) 📝

**Definition:** Chemical lightening of vital teeth using peroxide-based agents to improve esthetics.

**Mechanism:**
```
├── Peroxide Action: Hydrogen peroxide or carbamide peroxide breaks down into free radicals.
├── Oxidation: Free radicals oxidize chromogenic compounds in enamel and dentin.
├── Result: Lighter tooth color; removal of intrinsic and extrinsic stains.
└:: Depth of Action: Penetrates enamel to affect dentin stains.
```

**Methods of Vital Bleaching:**

*A. In-Office (Professional) Bleaching:*
```
├── Agents: 25-40% hydrogen peroxide.
├── Activation: Light, heat, or chemical activation to enhance efficacy.
├── Protocol:
│   ├── Isolation: Rubber dam or protective barrier for gingiva.
│   ├── Application: Apply gel to facial surfaces; avoid soft tissues.
│   ├── Activation: Light/heat × 15-20 minutes per cycle.
│   ├── Reapplication: 2-3 cycles per session.
│   └:: Post-treatment: Desensitizing agent; fluoride application.
│
├── Advantages:
│   ├── Immediate results; significant shade change in one visit.
│   ├── Controlled environment; professional supervision.
│   └:: Effective for severe discoloration.
│
├── Disadvantages:
│   ├── Higher cost; requires dental visit.
│   ├── Increased sensitivity risk; gingival irritation possible.
│   └:: Results may regress over time; maintenance needed.
│
└:: Examples: Zoom, Opalescence Boost, BriteSmile.
```

*B. At-Home (Night Guard) Bleaching:*
```
├── Agents: 10-22% carbamide peroxide (equivalent to 3.5-7.5% hydrogen peroxide).
├── Protocol:
│   ├── Custom Tray: Impressions for well-fitting bleaching tray.
│   ├── Application: Place small amount of gel in tray; wear 2-8 hours/day.
│   ├── Duration: 2-6 weeks for optimal results.
│   ├── Monitoring: Weekly check-ins for sensitivity, progress.
│   └:: Maintenance: Touch-up treatments as needed.
│
├── Advantages:
│   ├── Lower cost; convenient; patient-controlled.
│   ├── Lower sensitivity risk; gradual, natural-looking results.
│   └:: Good long-term stability with maintenance.
│
├── Disadvantages:
│   ├── Slower results; requires patient compliance.
│   ├── Tray fabrication required; initial dental visit needed.
│   └:: Less effective for severe discoloration vs. in-office.
│
└:: Examples: Opalescence, Nite White, Day White.
```

*C. Over-the-Counter (OTC) Products:*
```
├── Agents: 3-10% hydrogen peroxide; lower concentrations.
├── Forms: Strips, gels, paints, toothpastes.
├── Advantages: Readily available; low cost; no dental visit required.
├:: Disadvantages: Less effective; ill-fitting trays; higher risk of misuse.
└:: Examples: Crest Whitestrips, various drugstore products.
```

**Indications:**
```
├── Extrinsic Stains: Coffee, tea, tobacco, food pigments.
├── Intrinsic Stains: Mild tetracycline staining, fluorosis, age-related yellowing.
├── Generalized Discoloration: Overall lightening for esthetic enhancement.
├── Pre-Restorative: Lighten teeth before placing veneers/crowns for better shade match.
└:: Patient Request: Esthetic improvement for psychological well-being.
```

**Contraindications:**
```
├── Severe Tetracycline Staining: May require veneers/crowns for acceptable results.
├── Exposed Root Surfaces: Increased sensitivity risk; cervical erosion.
├── Pregnancy/Lactation: Relative contraindication; limited safety data.
├── Allergy to Peroxide: Rare but possible; alternative treatments needed.
├── Unrealistic Expectations: Demanding perfect white beyond natural limits.
└:: Active Caries or Periodontal Disease: Must be treated before bleaching.
```

**Side Effects & Management:**

*Tooth Sensitivity:*
```
├── Cause: Peroxide penetration to pulp; transient inflammation.
├── Prevention:
│   ├── Lower concentration; shorter application time.
│   ├── Desensitizing agents (potassium nitrate) pre/post treatment.
│   └:: Alternate-day treatment schedule.
│
├── Management:
│   ├── Discontinue treatment temporarily; resume at lower frequency.
│   ├── Apply desensitizing gel in tray; fluoride varnish application.
│   └:: NSAIDs for pain management if needed.
│
└:: Prognosis: Usually transient; resolves within 24-48 hours after treatment.
```

*Gingival Irritation:*
```
├── Cause: Peroxide contact with soft tissues.
├── Prevention:
│   ├── Proper isolation; avoid overfilling trays.
│   ├── Wipe excess gel from gingiva after application.
│   └:: Use well-fitting custom trays vs. OTC products.
│
├── Management:
│   ├── Discontinue treatment; rinse with water.
│   ├── Topical vitamin E or corticosteroid gel if severe.
│   └:: Usually self-limiting; resolves within hours.
│
└:: Prognosis: Mild irritation common; severe reactions rare with proper technique.
```

*Enamel Surface Changes:*
```
├── Cause: High-concentration peroxide; prolonged exposure.
├── Prevention:
│   ├── Follow manufacturer guidelines for concentration and time.
│   ├── Avoid over-bleaching; stop when desired shade achieved.
│   └:: Professional supervision for in-office treatments.
│
├── Management:
│   ├── Remineralizing agents (fluoride, CPP-ACP) post-treatment.
│   ├── Avoid abrasive toothpastes during and after bleaching.
│   └:: Monitor enamel surface; address roughness if present.
│
└:: Prognosis: Minimal changes with proper technique; reversible with remineralization.
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
```
├── Efficacy:
│   ├── In-office: 2-8 shade improvement in one visit.
│   ├── At-home: 2-6 shade improvement over 2-6 weeks.
│   └:: OTC: 1-3 shade improvement; variable results.
│
├── Longevity:
│   ├── Results stable 1-3 years with proper maintenance.
│   ├── Touch-up treatments maintain shade long-term.
│   └:: Lifestyle factors (diet, smoking) affect longevity.
│
├── Safety:
│   ├── No evidence of enamel damage with proper use.
│   ├── Sensitivity is most common side effect; usually transient.
│   └:: Long-term studies show no adverse effects on pulp vitality.
│
└:: Patient Satisfaction:
    ├── 80-90% satisfaction with professional bleaching.
    ├── Higher satisfaction with gradual at-home results vs. immediate in-office.
    └:: Realistic expectations critical for satisfaction.
```

**Clinical Protocol for At-Home Bleaching:**
```
1.  Pre-Treatment Assessment:
    ├── Medical/dental history; shade documentation; photos.
    ├── Rule out contraindications; treat caries/periodontal disease.
    ├── Discuss expectations; obtain informed consent.
    └:: Baseline sensitivity assessment.

2.  Tray Fabrication:
    ├── Impressions for custom bleaching tray.
    ├── Trim tray 1mm short of gingival margin to minimize gel contact.
    ├── Verify fit; adjust for comfort and retention.
    └:: Instruct patient on tray care and storage.

3.  Bleaching Phase:
    ├── Application: Pea-sized amount per tooth; wipe excess from gingiva.
    ├── Wear Time: 2-4 hours daytime OR overnight (follow product guidelines).
    ├── Duration: 2-6 weeks; monitor progress weekly.
    ├── Sensitivity Management: Potassium nitrate gel; alternate-day schedule if needed.
    └:: Documentation: Shade tracking; patient compliance monitoring.

4.  Completion:
    ├── Final shade assessment; compare to baseline.
    ├── Retention protocol: Touch-up treatments as needed.
    ├── Post-bleaching care: Avoid staining foods/drinks × 48h; maintain oral hygiene.
    └:: Follow-up: 1-month recall to assess stability; address concerns.
```

*Note: Vital bleaching is a safe, effective esthetic treatment when properly indicated and executed. Understanding mechanisms, methods, and management of side effects ensures predictable, satisfying outcomes for patients seeking tooth lightening.*

---

### 2. Non Carious destruction of teeth - 4m (437) 📝

**Definition:** Loss of tooth structure not caused by bacterial caries, resulting from mechanical, chemical, or stress-related factors.

**Classification:**

*A. Attrition:*
```
├── Definition: Tooth-to-tooth wear from function or parafunction.
├── Etiology:
│   ├── Physiological: Normal wear with aging; gradual flattening of incisal/occlusal surfaces.
│   ├── Pathological: Bruxism, clenching, malocclusion, abrasive diet.
│   └:: Iatrogenic: Poorly adjusted restorations causing premature contacts.
│
├── Clinical Features:
│   ├── Location: Incisal edges, occlusal surfaces, cusp tips.
│   ├── Appearance: Flattened surfaces; matching wear patterns on opposing teeth.
│   ├── Symptoms: Sensitivity if dentin exposed; TMJ symptoms if occlusion altered.
│   └:: Advanced cases: Reduced vertical dimension; esthetic changes.
│
├── Management:
│   ├── Occlusal analysis: Identify interferences; adjust as needed.
│   ├── Night guard: For bruxism/clenching to prevent further wear.
│   ├── Restoration: Composite, onlays, crowns to restore form/function if significant loss.
│   └:: Full-mouth rehabilitation: For severe attrition with vertical dimension loss.
```

*B. Abrasion:*
```
├── Definition: Loss of tooth structure from mechanical wear by foreign objects.
├── Etiology:
│   ├── Toothbrush abrasion: Aggressive brushing, hard bristles, abrasive toothpaste.
│   ├── Occupational: Holding nails/pins between teeth, pipe smoking.
│   ├── Habits: Nail-biting, pen-chewing, toothpick misuse.
│   └:: Iatrogenic: Improper use of dental instruments, abrasive prophylaxis pastes.
│
├── Clinical Features:
│   ├── Location: Facial cervical areas (toothbrush); incisal edges (habits).
│   ├── Shape: Saucer-shaped, shallow, wide defects with smooth surfaces.
│   ├── Surface: Polished, hard, often sensitive due to dentin exposure.
│   └:: Pattern: Often affects multiple teeth in similar locations.
│
├── Management:
│   ├── Eliminate cause: Soft toothbrush, gentle technique, habit counseling.
│   ├── Desensitizing agents: Potassium nitrate, fluoride varnish for sensitivity.
│   ├── Restoration: GIC/composite if defect deep, sensitive, or esthetically concerning.
│   └:: Prevention: Patient education on proper oral hygiene techniques.
```

*C. Erosion:*
```
├── Definition: Chemical dissolution of tooth structure by acids not of bacterial origin.
├── Etiology:
│   ├── Extrinsic: Dietary acids (citrus, carbonated drinks, wine), occupational exposure, medications.
│   ├── Intrinsic: GERD, bulimia, pregnancy-related morning sickness, chronic alcoholism.
│   └:: Lifestyle: Swimming in chlorinated pools, recreational drug use.
│
├── Clinical Features:
│   ├── Location: Palatal surfaces of maxillary anteriors (intrinsic); facial surfaces of mandibular anteriors (extrinsic).
│   ├── Appearance: Smooth, shiny, "cupped-out" lesions; incisal edges become thin, translucent.
│   ├── Symptoms: Sensitivity, esthetic concerns, functional impairment in advanced cases.
│   └:: Advanced cases: Severe tooth structure loss; occlusal changes.
│
├── Management:
│   ├── Identify & eliminate source: Dietary counseling, medical referral for GERD/eating disorders.
│   ├── Preventive measures: Fluoride, CPP-ACP, saliva stimulation.
│   ├── Restorative intervention: GIC/composite for cervical defects; crowns for advanced cases.
│   └:: Interdisciplinary care: Medical management of underlying conditions.
```

*D. Abfraction:*
```
├── Definition: Loss of tooth structure at cervical region due to biomechanical loading forces causing flexure and fatigue failure.
├── Etiology:
│   ├── Occlusal stress → tooth flexure → tensile/compressive forces at cervical area.
│   ├── Risk factors: Bruxism, malocclusion, Class II div 1 malocclusion, heavy occlusal contacts.
│   └:: Often coexists with abrasion/erosion ("combined cervical lesion").
│
├── Clinical Features:
│   ├── Location: Cervical area, typically at/below CEJ.
│   ├── Shape: Wedge-shaped or V-shaped defect with sharp line angles.
│   ├── Surface: Smooth, shiny, hard (distinguishes from abrasion/erosion).
│   └:: Symptoms: Sensitivity; predisposes to caries, erosion, abrasion.
│
├── Management:
│   ├── Address occlusal factors: Occlusal adjustment, night guard for bruxism.
│   ├── Restore if: Sensitivity, caries risk, esthetic concern, progressive loss.
│   ├── Material choice: GIC/RMGIC for cervical lesions (fluoride release, adhesion to dentin).
│   └:: Prevention: Occlusal analysis, stress management, proper brushing technique.
```

**Differential Diagnosis:**

| Feature | Attrition | Abrasion | Erosion | Abfraction |
|---------|-----------|----------|---------|------------|
| **Cause** | Tooth-to-tooth contact | Foreign object friction | Chemical dissolution | Occlusal stress/flexure |
| **Location** | Incisal/occlusal surfaces | Facial cervical; incisal edges | Palatal (intrinsic); facial (extrinsic) | Cervical at CEJ |
| **Shape** | Flattened surfaces | Saucer-shaped, shallow | Smooth, cupped-out | Wedge/V-shaped, sharp angles |
| **Surface** | Smooth, matching opposing tooth | Polished, hard | Smooth, satin-like | Smooth, shiny, hard |
| **Sensitivity** | If dentin exposed | Common due to dentin exposure | Common due to dentin exposure | Common due to dentin exposure |
| **Management** | Occlusal adjustment, night guard | Eliminate cause, restore if needed | Eliminate acid source, restore | Address occlusion, restore if needed |

**Comprehensive Management Approach:**
```
├── Diagnosis:
│   ├── Detailed history: Diet, habits, medical conditions, parafunction.
│   ├── Clinical examination: Location, shape, surface characteristics of lesions.
│   ├── Radiographic assessment: Rule out caries, periapical pathology.
│   └:: Differential diagnosis: Distinguish between attrition, abrasion, erosion, abfraction.
│
├── Etiology Management:
│   ├── Behavioral modification: Brushing technique, dietary counseling, habit cessation.
│   ├── Medical referral: GERD, eating disorders, xerostomia management.
│   ├── Occlusal management: Night guard for bruxism; occlusal adjustment.
│   └:: Preventive measures: Fluoride, CPP-ACP, saliva stimulation.
│
├── Restorative Intervention:
│   ├── Material selection: GIC/RMGIC for cervical lesions; composite for esthetic zones.
│   ├── Minimally invasive: Preserve sound tooth structure; restore only if necessary.
│   ├── Marginal placement: Supragingival when possible; respect biological width.
│   └:: Finishing: Smooth margins; proper contour for plaque control.
│
├── Monitoring & Maintenance:
│   ├── Regular recall: Monitor lesion progression, restoration integrity.
│   ├── Patient education: Reinforce etiology management; oral hygiene instruction.
│   └:: Interdisciplinary care: Coordinate with physicians, periodontists as needed.
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
```
├── Prevention: Addressing etiology reduces progression in 70-80% of cases.
├── Restoration Longevity: GIC/RMGIC cervical restorations show 5-8 year survival.
├── Sensitivity Management: Desensitizing agents reduce sensitivity in 60-80% of cases.
└:: Patient Satisfaction: High when etiology addressed and esthetics improved.
```

*Note: Non-carious tooth destruction requires comprehensive diagnosis and management addressing both the defect and underlying etiology. A multidisciplinary approach often yields the best long-term outcomes.*

---

### 3. Cervical lesions - 4m 📝

**Definition:** Loss of tooth structure at the cervical region (near CEJ) from various etiologies including abrasion, erosion, abfraction, or caries.

**Etiology:**
```
├── Non-Carious Cervical Lesions (NCCL):
│   ├── Abrasion: Toothbrush trauma; aggressive brushing technique.
│   ├── Erosion: Acid dissolution from dietary or gastric sources.
│   ├── Abfraction: Occlusal stress causing cervical flexure and fatigue.
│   └:: Often multifactorial: Combination of above factors.
│
├── Carious Cervical Lesions:
│   ├── Root caries: Exposed root surfaces in elderly or periodontally compromised patients.
│   ├── Cervical caries: At CEJ in patients with poor oral hygiene, high caries risk.
│   └:: Management differs from NCCL; requires caries removal and restoration.
```

**Clinical Features:**
```
├── Location: Facial or lingual cervical areas; often at/below CEJ.
├── Appearance:
│   ├── Abrasion: Saucer-shaped, shallow, smooth surface.
│   ├── Erosion: Smooth, shiny, "cupped-out" appearance.
│   ├── Abfraction: Wedge-shaped, V-shaped with sharp line angles.
│   └:: Caries: Soft, discolored, cavitated; may have plaque accumulation.
│
├── Symptoms:
│   ├── Sensitivity: Common due to dentin exposure; triggered by cold, sweet, brushing.
│   ├── Esthetic concerns: Visible defects in smile zone.
│   ├── Functional impairment: If deep, may affect tooth strength.
│   └:: Often asymptomatic: Discovered during routine examination.
│
├── Progression:
│   ├── Slow progression if etiology addressed; rapid if ongoing.
│   ├── May lead to pulp exposure if deep and untreated.
│   └:: Can predispose to caries in exposed dentin.
```

**Diagnosis:**
```
├── History:
│   ├── Brushing habits: Frequency, technique, toothbrush type.
│   ├── Dietary habits: Acidic foods/drinks; frequency of consumption.
│   ├── Medical history: GERD, bulimia, xerostomia, medications.
│   ├── Parafunction: Bruxism, clenching; night guard use.
│   └:: Occupation/hobbies: Acid exposure, tooth use as tool.
│
├── Clinical Examination:
│   ├── Visual: Location, shape, surface characteristics of lesion.
│   ├── Tactile: Explorer to assess hardness; differentiate caries vs. NCCL.
│   ├── Sensitivity testing: Cold, air, explorer to assess dentin exposure.
│   ├── Periodontal assessment: Gingival recession, pocket depths, mobility.
│   └:: Occlusal analysis: Premature contacts, wear facets, parafunction signs.
│
├── Radiographic Assessment:
│   ├── Periapical radiographs: Rule out caries, periapical pathology.
│   ├── Bitewings: Assess proximal caries; cervical lesion extent.
│   └:: Limitations: Cervical lesions often not visible radiographically.
│
└:: Differential Diagnosis:
    ├── Caries vs. NCCL: Hardness on exploration; radiographic evidence.
    ├── Abrasion vs. erosion vs. abfraction: Shape, surface characteristics, etiology.
    └:: Rule out other pathologies: Resorption, fracture, cyst.
```

**Management:**

*A. Preventive & Etiology Management:*
```
├── Behavioral Modification:
│   ├── Brushing technique: Soft brush, gentle pressure, non-abrasive toothpaste.
│   ├── Dietary counseling: Limit acidic foods/drinks; rinse with water after consumption.
│   ├── Timing: Wait 30-60 minutes after acid exposure before brushing.
│   └:: Habit cessation: Nail-biting, pen-chewing, occupational habits.
│
├── Medical Management:
│   ├── GERD/eating disorders: Referral to physician; medical treatment.
│   ├── Xerostomia: Saliva substitutes, sugar-free gum, medication review.
│   └:: Medication review: Identify acidogenic medications; alternatives if possible.
│
├── Occlusal Management:
│   ├── Night guard: For bruxism/clenching to reduce occlusal stress.
│   ├── Occlusal adjustment: Eliminate premature contacts contributing to abfraction.
│   └:: Stress management: Address parafunction triggers.
│
└:: Preventive Agents:
    ├── Fluoride: High-concentration toothpaste (5000 ppm); varnish applications.
    ├── CPP-ACP: Casein phosphopeptide-amorphous calcium phosphate for remineralization.
    ├── Desensitizing agents: Potassium nitrate, stannous fluoride for sensitivity.
    └:: Saliva stimulation: Hydration, sugar-free gum for xerostomia patients.
```

*B. Restorative Intervention:*

*Indications for Restoration:*
```
├── Sensitivity: Unresponsive to desensitizing agents.
├── Esthetic Concerns: Visible defects in smile zone.
├── Caries Risk: Exposed dentin prone to caries; high caries risk patient.
├── Progressive Loss: Lesion deepening despite etiology management.
├── Structural Compromise: Deep lesion threatening tooth strength.
└:: Patient Request: Esthetic or functional improvement desired.
```

*Material Selection:*
```
├── Glass Ionomer Cement (GIC):
│   ├── Advantages: Adhesion to dentin; fluoride release; biocompatibility.
│   ├── Disadvantages: Lower strength; poor esthetics; moisture sensitivity.
│   └:: Best for: Low-stress areas; high caries risk; moisture-challenged situations.
│
├── Resin-Modified GIC (RMGIC):
│   ├── Advantages: Improved strength/esthetics vs. GIC; less moisture sensitive.
│   ├── Disadvantages: Still not as strong as composite; HEMA sensitivity risk.
│   └:: Best for: Moderate-stress cervical lesions; better esthetics needed.
│
├── Composite Resin:
│   ├── Advantages: Superior esthetics; good strength; versatile.
│   ├── Disadvantages: Technique-sensitive; requires excellent isolation.
│   └:: Best for: Esthetic zones; patients demanding optimal appearance.
│
└:: Flowable Composite:
    ├── Advantages: Excellent adaptation; easy placement in cervical areas.
    ├── Disadvantages: Lower strength; not for high-stress areas.
    └:: Best for: Small lesions; liner under universal composite.
```

*Restoration Protocol:*
```
1.  Preparation:
    ├── Conservative: Remove only caries/undermined enamel; preserve sound structure.
    ├── Surface conditioning: Pumice cleaning; avoid desiccation of dentin.
    ├── Margin design: Feather-edge or minimal bevel for composite; butt joint for GIC.
    └:: Pulp protection: Liner if deep; GIC itself provides some protection.

2.  Isolation:
    ├── Rubber dam preferred; cotton rolls + high-volume suction if dam not feasible.
    ├── Gingival retraction: Cord if subgingival margin present.
    └:: Moisture control: Critical for bonding success with composite.

3.  Bonding (for composite/RMGIC):
    ├── Etching: 37% phosphoric acid × 15 sec enamel, 10 sec dentin.
    ├── Rinsing/Drying: Frosty enamel; moist dentin.
    ├── Adhesive: Apply with rubbing motion; air thin; light cure.
    └:: Verification: Glossy surface; no voids.

4.  Placement:
    ├── GIC: Mix per manufacturer; place incrementally; adapt to cavity walls.
    ├── Composite: Incremental placement; 2mm layers; light cure each increment.
    ├── Contouring: Reproduce natural cervical contour; avoid over-contouring.
    └:: Protective coating: For conventional GIC; apply immediately after placement.

5.  Finishing:
    ├── GIC: Wait 24 hours for final set; finish with fine diamonds, carbides.
    ├── Composite: Finish immediately; sequential abrasives for smooth surface.
    ├── Marginal refinement: Smooth transition from restoration to tooth.
    └:: Polishing: High-gloss finish for plaque resistance; natural appearance.
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
```
├── Longevity:
│   ├── GIC: 3-5 years in low-stress cervical lesions.
│   ├── RMGIC: 5-8 years in moderate-stress lesions.
│   ├── Composite: 7-10 years with proper technique and isolation.
│   └:: Success depends on etiology management as much as restoration quality.
│
├── Success Factors:
│   ├── Proper diagnosis and etiology management.
│   ├── Appropriate material selection for clinical situation.
│   ├── Meticulous technique: Isolation, bonding, placement, finishing.
│   └:: Patient compliance with preventive measures and oral hygiene.
│
├── Failure Modes:
│   ├── Marginal breakdown (40%): Often due to ongoing etiology or poor technique.
│   ├── Sensitivity (30%): Inadequate sealing of dentinal tubules.
│   ├── Recurrent caries (20%): Poor oral hygiene; high caries risk.
│   └:: Esthetic dissatisfaction (10%): Shade mismatch; poor contour.
```

**Common Errors & Prevention:**
```
❌ Inadequate etiology management: Restoration fails due to ongoing abrasion/erosion.
✅ Solution: Address underlying cause before restoration; patient education.

❌ Poor isolation: Moisture contamination compromises bonding.
✅ Solution: Rubber dam mandatory for composite; cotton rolls + suction for GIC.

❌ Over-preparation: Unnecessary tooth removal; weakens tooth.
✅ Solution: Conservative approach; remove only caries/undermined structure.

❌ Poor marginal adaptation: Microleakage; sensitivity; recurrent caries.
✅ Solution: Precise placement; proper finishing; smooth margins.

❌ Over-contouring: Plaque retention; gingival inflammation.
✅ Solution: Reproduce natural cervical anatomy; smooth transitions.
```

*Note: Cervical lesions require comprehensive management addressing both the defect and underlying etiology. Material selection should be based on clinical demands, with GIC/RMGIC preferred for moisture-challenged or high caries risk situations, and composite for optimal esthetics in visible areas.*

---

### 4. Minimal Intervention Dentistry - 4m (449) 📝

**Definition:** Philosophy and approach to dental care that focuses on prevention, early detection, and minimally invasive treatment of dental diseases, preserving maximum tooth structure.

**Core Principles:**
```
├── Prevention First:
│   ├── Primary prevention: Fluoride, sealants, diet counseling to prevent disease.
│   ├── Secondary prevention: Early detection and intervention to halt progression.
│   └:: Tertiary prevention: Minimally invasive treatment to preserve function.
│
├── Early Detection:
│   ├── Advanced diagnostics: Laser fluorescence, QLF, CBCT for early caries detection.
│   ├── Risk assessment: CAMBRA, Cariogram for personalized prevention plans.
│   └:: Regular monitoring: Shorter recall intervals for high-risk patients.
│
├── Minimally Invasive Treatment:
│   ├── Selective caries removal: Remove only infected dentin; preserve affected dentin.
│   ├── Conservative cavity design: No extension for prevention; preserve sound structure.
│   ├── Adhesive dentistry: Bonding allows minimal mechanical retention.
│   └:: Repair vs. replacement: Repair defective margins vs. replacing entire restoration.
│
└:: Patient-Centered Care:
    ├── Education: Empower patients with knowledge for self-care.
    ├── Shared decision-making: Involve patients in treatment planning.
    └:: Long-term partnership: Focus on lifelong oral health, not just episodic treatment.
```

**Clinical Applications:**

*Caries Management:*
```
├── Non-Operative Approaches:
│   ├── Fluoride therapy: High-concentration toothpaste, varnish applications.
│   ├── Sealants: Pit and fissure sealants for caries prevention.
│   ├── Antimicrobials: Chlorhexidine, xylitol for high-risk patients.
│   └:: Remineralization: CPP-ACP, bioactive materials to reverse early lesions.
│
├── Minimally Invasive Restorations:
│   ├── Selective caries removal: Preserve affected dentin under seal.
│   ├── Tunnel preparation: Access proximal caries through occlusal; preserve marginal ridge.
│   ├── Preventive resin restoration: Sealant + minimal composite for early occlusal caries.
│   └:: ART: Hand instruments + HVGIC for resource-limited settings.
│
└:: Monitoring:
    ├── Regular recall: Monitor lesion activity; intervene if progression.
    ├── Radiographic monitoring: Bitewings to assess proximal lesions.
    └:: Patient compliance: Essential for non-operative approaches to succeed.
```

*Restorative Dentistry:*
```
├── Conservative Cavity Design:
│   ├── No extension for prevention: Remove only caries/defective structure.
│   ├── Enamel bevels: Enhance bonding; preserve tooth structure.
│   ├── Rounded internal angles: Reduce stress concentration.
│   └:: Adequate but minimal bulk: Preserve tooth strength.
│
├── Adhesive Techniques:
│   ├── Etch-and-rinse or self-etch adhesives for enamel/dentin bonding.
│   ├── Incremental placement: Manage polymerization shrinkage; reduce stress.
│   ├── Layering: Mimic natural tooth structure for esthetics and function.
│   └:: Repair protocols: Bond new composite to existing restoration when possible.
│
├── Material Selection:
│   ├── Bioactive materials: GIC, RMGIC for fluoride release, adhesion.
│   ├── Nanofilled composites: Strength + esthetics for conservative restorations.
│   ├── Bulk-fill composites: Time efficiency with minimal shrinkage stress.
│   └:: Biomimetic approaches: Mimic natural tooth properties and behavior.
```

*Periodontal Considerations:*
```
├── Minimally Invasive Periodontal Therapy:
│   ├── Scaling and root planing: Conservative debridement vs. surgical approaches.
│   ├── Local antimicrobials: Targeted delivery to periodontal pockets.
│   ├── Regenerative procedures: Preserve bone and attachment when possible.
│   └:: Maintenance: Regular supportive periodontal therapy.
│
├── Cervical Lesion Management:
│   ├── Etiology management: Address abrasion, erosion, abfraction causes.
│   ├── Conservative restoration: GIC/RMGIC for adhesion and fluoride release.
│   ├── Minimal preparation: Preserve sound tooth structure.
│   └:: Prevention: Patient education on brushing technique, diet, occlusion.
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
```
├── Caries Prevention:
│   ├── Fluoride: 20-40% reduction in caries incidence.
│   ├── Sealants: 70-80% reduction in occlusal caries.
│   ├── Risk-based care: 40-60% reduction in caries in high-risk populations.
│   └:: Early intervention: 50% reduction in need for extensive restorations.
│
├── Restorative Longevity:
│   ├── Minimally invasive preparations: 15-20% longer restoration survival.
│   ├── Adhesive techniques: 30-40% reduction in marginal breakdown.
│   ├── Repair vs. replacement: 50% cost savings with equivalent outcomes.
│   └:: Patient satisfaction: Higher with conservative, tooth-preserving approaches.
│
├── Cost-Effectiveness:
│   ├── Prevention: $1 saved in restorative costs for every $0.50 spent on prevention.
│   ├── Early intervention: 30-50% lower treatment costs vs. delayed care.
│   └:: Long-term value: Preserved tooth structure reduces future treatment needs.
```

**Implementation in Clinical Practice:**
```
├── Risk Assessment:
│   ├── CAMBRA (Caries Management by Risk Assessment): Categorize patients by risk.
│   ├── Personalized prevention plans: Tailored to individual risk factors.
│   └:: Recall intervals: Based on risk, not arbitrary timeframes.
│
├── Diagnostic Tools:
│   ├── Laser fluorescence (DIAGNOdent): Quantitative caries detection.
│   ├── QLF (Quantitative Light-Induced Fluorescence): Monitor remineralization.
│   ├── CBCT: 3D assessment for complex cases; use judiciously due to radiation.
│   └:: Digital monitoring: Track changes over time with digital radiographs/photos.
│
├── Treatment Planning:
│   ├── Stepwise approach: Start with least invasive option; escalate as needed.
│   ├── Shared decision-making: Discuss options, risks, benefits with patient.
│   └:: Long-term perspective: Plan for lifelong oral health, not just immediate needs.
│
└:: Patient Education:
    ├── Oral hygiene instruction: Proper brushing, flossing, interdental cleaning.
    ├── Dietary counseling: Limit sugar frequency; acidic food/drink management.
    └:: Empowerment: Equip patients with knowledge and tools for self-care.
```

**Challenges & Solutions:**
```
├── Reimbursement:
│   ├── Challenge: Insurance may not cover preventive/minimally invasive procedures.
│   ├── Solution: Document medical necessity; advocate for policy changes.
│   └:: Patient communication: Explain long-term value of preventive care.
│
├── Time Management:
│   ├── Challenge: Prevention and education require more chair time.
│   ├── Solution: Delegate to hygienists; use team-based care approach.
│   └:: Efficiency: Digital tools for risk assessment, monitoring, documentation.
│
├── Patient Compliance:
│   ├── Challenge: Behavioral change difficult; prevention requires ongoing effort.
│   ├── Solution: Motivational interviewing; set achievable goals; regular reinforcement.
│   └:: Technology: Apps, reminders, telehealth for ongoing support.
│
└:: Evidence Integration:
    ├── Challenge: Rapidly evolving evidence; keeping up with literature.
    ├── Solution: Continuing education; evidence-based guidelines; critical appraisal skills.
    └:: Clinical judgment: Balance evidence with individual patient needs and preferences.
```

*Conclusion: Minimal Intervention Dentistry represents a paradigm shift from traditional "drill and fill" to a preventive, conservative, patient-centered approach. By focusing on early detection, risk-based care, and minimally invasive treatment, MID preserves tooth structure, reduces treatment costs, and improves long-term oral health outcomes. Successful implementation requires commitment to prevention, adoption of advanced diagnostics, and partnership with patients in their oral health journey.*

---

### 5. Air abrasion - 2m (451) 📝

**Definition:** Minimally invasive technique using a stream of abrasive particles (typically aluminum oxide) propelled by compressed air to remove tooth structure, caries, or old restorations.

**Equipment:**
```
├── Handpiece: Delivers controlled stream of abrasive particles.
├── Abrasive Media: Aluminum oxide (50μm most common); other options include silica, bicarbonate.
├── Air Source: Compressed air or nitrogen; regulated pressure (20-100 psi).
├── Suction: High-volume evacuation essential to capture debris and abrasive.
└:: Safety: Protective eyewear for patient and operator; rubber dam for isolation.
```

**Applications:**
```
├── Caries Removal:
│   ├── Selective removal of infected dentin; preserve affected dentin.
│   ├── Minimally invasive; preserves sound tooth structure.
│   └:: Particularly useful for small occlusal and proximal caries.
│
├── Cavity Preparation:
│   ├── Conservative preparations for small restorations.
│   ├── No vibration or heat; patient comfort.
│   └:: Ideal for preventive resin restorations, sealant placement.
│
├── Surface Preparation:
│   ├── Etching enamel for bonding; micro-roughening for adhesion.
│   ├── Cleaning tooth surfaces before bonding or sealing.
│   └:: Removing stains, plaque, pellicle without damaging tooth structure.
│
├── Restoration Repair:
│   ├── Removing defective margins; preparing surface for repair bonding.
│   ├── Minimal removal of sound tooth structure.
│   └:: Preserving existing restoration when possible.
│
└:: Other Uses:
    ├── Removing old cement from crowns/bridges.
    ├── Preparing implants for impression or abutment placement.
    └:: Cosmetic procedures: Enameloplasty, minor tooth reshaping.
```

**Advantages:**
```
├── Minimally Invasive: Preserves maximum sound tooth structure.
├── Patient Comfort: No vibration, heat, or noise; often no anesthesia needed.
├── Precision: Controlled removal; selective caries removal possible.
├:: Efficiency: Quick for small preparations; no bur changes.
├:: Bonding Enhancement: Creates micro-retentive surface for improved adhesion.
└:: Versatility: Multiple applications beyond caries removal.
```

**Disadvantages:**
```
├── Limited to Small Lesions: Not efficient for large caries or preparations.
├── Aerosol Generation: Requires high-volume evacuation; infection control considerations.
├── Learning Curve: Technique-sensitive; requires practice for optimal results.
├:: Cost: Equipment investment; ongoing abrasive media costs.
├:: Noise: Compressor noise may be concerning for some patients.
└:: Not for All Materials: Less effective on metal restorations; limited on dense dentin.
```

**Clinical Protocol:**
```
1.  Isolation: Rubber dam preferred; high-volume evacuation essential.
2.  Safety: Protective eyewear for patient and operator.
3.  Settings: Adjust pressure (20-100 psi) and abrasive flow for application.
4.  Technique:
    ├── Hold handpiece 1-2mm from tooth surface; 45° angle.
    ├── Use brushing or circular motion; avoid prolonged focus on one spot.
    ├── Intermittent application; allow cooling and debris removal.
    └:: Monitor progress; stop when desired endpoint achieved.
5.  Cleaning: Rinse thoroughly; remove all abrasive residue.
6.  Verification: Explorer to assess preparation; radiograph if needed.
```

*Note: Air abrasion is a valuable adjunct for minimally invasive dentistry, particularly for small caries removal and surface preparation. Proper technique and case selection are essential for optimal outcomes.*

---

### 6. Carisolv - 2m (453) 📝

**Definition:** Chemomechanical caries removal system using sodium hypochlorite and amino acids to selectively dissolve infected dentin.

**Mechanism:**
```
├── Chemical Action:
│   ├── Sodium hypochlorite: Oxidizes and denatures collagen in infected dentin.
│   ├── Amino acids (glutamic acid, leucine): Enhance selectivity for infected tissue.
│   └:: Result: Infected dentin softened; affected dentin remains firm.
│
├── Mechanical Action:
│   ├── Special hand instruments: Curettes designed to remove softened dentin.
│   ├── Gentle scraping motion; preserves sound tooth structure.
│   └:: No vibration or heat; patient comfort.
```

**Clinical Protocol:**
```
1.  Application: Apply Carisolv gel to carious lesion.
2.  Waiting Period: Allow 30 seconds for chemical action.
3.  Removal: Use special curettes to remove softened infected dentin.
4.  Reapplication: Repeat steps 1-3 until firm, unaffected dentin reached.
5.  Rinsing: Thoroughly rinse to remove all gel residue.
6.  Restoration: Proceed with bonding and restoration as usual.
```

**Advantages:**
```
├── Selective Caries Removal: Preserves affected dentin; minimally invasive.
├── Patient Comfort: No vibration, heat, or noise; often no anesthesia needed.
├:: Quiet Operation: Ideal for anxious patients, pediatric dentistry.
├:: Preservation: Maximum sound tooth structure; reduced pulp exposure risk.
└:: Bonding: Creates clean surface for adhesive bonding.
```

**Disadvantages:**
```
├── Time-Consuming: Slower than rotary instruments for caries removal.
├── Limited to Superficial Caries: Less effective for deep or hard caries.
├:: Cost: Special gel and instruments; ongoing material costs.
├:: Learning Curve: Technique-sensitive; requires practice.
└:: Availability: Not widely available in all regions.
```

**Indications:**
```
├── Small to moderate occlusal caries.
├── Pediatric patients; anxious patients; needle-phobic patients.
├── Minimally invasive approach; preserve maximum tooth structure.
├:: Patients with medical conditions contraindicating rotary instruments.
```

**Contraindications:**
```
├── Deep caries near pulp: Risk of exposure; limited control.
├── Hard, sclerotic dentin: Less effective; rotary instruments preferred.
├:: Large restorations: Time-inefficient for extensive caries removal.
```

*Note: Carisolv offers a chemomechanical alternative for selective caries removal, particularly valuable in minimally invasive and pediatric dentistry. While slower than rotary instruments, its selectivity and patient comfort make it a useful adjunct in appropriate clinical situations.*

---

### 7. Night-guard bleaching - 4m (485) 📝
🔗 *See Section 13, Question 1 for comprehensive vital bleaching discussion; this focuses on night-guard technique.*

**Definition:** At-home vital bleaching using custom-fabricated trays with low-concentration carbamide peroxide gel worn overnight or for several hours daily.

**Protocol:**

*Pre-Treatment:*
```
├── Assessment:
│   ├── Medical/dental history; shade documentation; photos.
│   ├── Rule out contraindications: Caries, periodontal disease, sensitivity.
│   ├── Discuss expectations; obtain informed consent.
│   └:: Baseline sensitivity assessment.
│
├── Tray Fabrication:
│   ├── Impressions: Alginate or PVS for accurate models.
│   ├── Model preparation: Trim to full arch; ensure no undercuts.
│   ├── Tray fabrication: Vacuum-formed 0.5-1.0mm thick tray.
│   ├── Trimming: Trim 1mm short of gingival margin to minimize gel contact.
│   └:: Verification: Check fit, comfort, retention; adjust as needed.
│
└:: Patient Education:
    ├── Tray care: Clean with toothbrush; store dry.
    ├── Gel application: Pea-sized amount per tooth; avoid overfilling.
    ├── Wear schedule: Follow product guidelines (typically 2-8 hours/day).
    └:: Sensitivity management: Potassium nitrate gel; alternate-day schedule if needed.
```

*Bleaching Phase:*
```
├── Application:
│   ├── Place small amount of gel (10-15% carbamide peroxide) in tray facial surfaces.
│   ├── Insert tray; ensure even distribution; wipe excess from gingiva.
│   └:: Wear time: 2-4 hours daytime OR overnight (8 hours); follow manufacturer.
│
├── Monitoring:
│   ├── Weekly check-ins: Assess progress, sensitivity, compliance.
│   ├── Shade tracking: Compare to baseline; document improvement.
│   ├── Sensitivity management: Desensitizing gel; adjust wear schedule if needed.
│   └:: Duration: Typically 2-6 weeks for optimal results.
│
└:: Completion:
    ├── Final shade assessment: Compare to baseline; document outcome.
    ├── Retention protocol: Touch-up treatments every 3-6 months as needed.
    ├── Post-bleaching care: Avoid staining foods/drinks × 48h; maintain oral hygiene.
    └:: Follow-up: 1-month recall to assess stability; address concerns.
```

**Advantages:**
```
├── Patient Control: Wear at convenient times; adjust frequency based on sensitivity.
├── Gradual Results: Natural-looking lightening; reduces rebound effect.
├:: Cost-Effective: Lower initial cost vs. in-office; reusable trays.
├:: Safety: Low concentration minimizes sensitivity, gingival irritation.
├:: Evidence: 70-90% patient satisfaction; shade improvement of 2-8 VITA shades.
└:: Convenience: At-home treatment; no dental visits during bleaching phase.
```

**Disadvantages:**
```
├── Compliance Dependent: Results depend on patient adherence to schedule.
├── Slower Results: Takes weeks vs. immediate in-office results.
├:: Tray Issues: Poor fit can cause gel leakage, gingival irritation.
├:: Sensitivity: Still possible; requires management protocol.
└:: Maintenance: Touch-up treatments needed to maintain results long-term.
```

**Sensitivity Management:**
```
├── Prevention:
│   ├── Lower concentration (10% carbamide peroxide) for sensitive patients.
│   ├── Shorter wear time initially; gradually increase as tolerated.
│   ├── Potassium nitrate gel in tray pre/post bleaching.
│   └:: Alternate-day schedule for first week.
│
├── Treatment:
│   ├── Discontinue temporarily; resume at lower frequency.
│   ├── Apply desensitizing gel in tray; fluoride varnish application.
│   ├── NSAIDs for pain management if needed.
│   └:: Usually resolves within 24-48 hours after treatment adjustment.
│
└:: Prognosis: Sensitivity is usually transient; rarely leads to treatment discontinuation.
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
```
├── Efficacy: 2-8 shade improvement on VITA scale; 70-90% patient satisfaction.
├── Longevity: Results stable 1-3 years with proper maintenance; touch-ups extend longevity.
├:: Safety: No evidence of enamel damage with proper use; sensitivity most common side effect.
├:: Cost-Effectiveness: Lower cost than in-office; good value for motivated patients.
└:: Patient Preference: Many prefer at-home control and gradual results.
```

*Note: Night-guard bleaching is the most popular and evidence-supported method for vital tooth bleaching, offering a balance of efficacy, safety, and patient control. Proper case selection, tray fabrication, and patient education are critical for successful outcomes.*

---

### 8. Walking-bleach tech - 4m (483) 📝

**Definition:** Intracoronal bleaching technique for non-vital discolored teeth using sodium perborate placed in pulp chamber and sealed temporarily ("walking" between visits).

**Indications:**
```
├── Discoloration of endodontically treated anterior teeth.
├── Intact enamel, no cervical defects or cracks.
├── Adequate root canal treatment with good apical seal.
├── Patient preference for conservative, non-invasive approach.
└:: Alternative to veneers/crowns for intrinsic discoloration.
```

**Contraindications:**
```
├── Cervical resorption, cracks, or defects (risk of bleach penetration).
├── Inadequate root canal seal (risk of peroxide diffusion to periapex).
├── Patient allergy to peroxide compounds.
├── Extensive coronal destruction requiring crown anyway.
└:: Unrealistic expectations for shade improvement.
```

**Materials:**
```
├── Bleaching Agent: Sodium perborate monohydrate (preferred) or carbamide peroxide.
├── Barrier: 2mm glass ionomer or resin-modified GIC over GP at CEJ.
├── Temporary Seal: Cavit or IRM for access closure.
├── Protective: Petroleum jelly on gingiva to prevent chemical burn.
└:: Mixing: Distilled water or saline to form thick paste.
```

**Step-by-Step Protocol:**
```
1.  Pre-Bleaching Assessment:
    ├── Confirm adequate RCT: Radiograph showing good obturation, no periapical pathology.
    ├── Rule out cervical defects: Explorer, radiograph, transillumination.
    ├── Shade documentation: Pre-op photos, shade guide recording.
    └:: Informed consent: Discuss procedure, expectations, risks.

2.  Access Preparation:
    ├── Remove existing restoration; access pulp chamber.
    ├── Remove GP to 2mm below CEJ; place cervical barrier (glass ionomer).
    ├── Critical: Barrier prevents bleach penetration to periodontium (resorption risk).
    └:: Verify barrier integrity: No gaps; complete seal at CEJ.

3.  Bleach Application:
    ├── Mix sodium perborate + distilled water/saline to thick paste.
    ├── Pack into pulp chamber; avoid contact with barrier/tooth structure.
    ├── Seal access with Cavit (3-4mm thickness); smooth margins.
    └:: Instruct patient: Avoid staining foods; report sensitivity immediately.

4.  Waiting Period:
    ├── Leave 3-7 days; instruct patient to avoid staining foods/drinks.
    ├── Monitor for sensitivity (rare with proper barrier).
    └:: Return for evaluation; repeat if needed (max 3-4 applications).

5.  Completion:
    ├── Remove bleach; irrigate chamber thoroughly.
    ├── Etch, bond, place composite restoration matching adjacent teeth.
    ├── Final shade verification; document outcome.
    └:: Post-op instructions: Normal oral hygiene; avoid staining foods × 48h.
```

**Mechanism:**
```
├── Sodium perborate releases hydrogen peroxide slowly.
├── Hydrogen peroxide oxidizes chromogenic compounds (hemoglobin breakdown products) in dentin.
├── Result: Lighter tooth color; removal of intrinsic stains from pulp necrosis.
└:: Controlled release: Slower than high-concentration H₂O₂; reduced resorption risk.
```

**Advantages:**
```
├── Safety: Lower concentration than in-office bleaching; lower external cervical resorption risk.
├── Efficacy: 80-90% success for mild-moderate discoloration.
├:: Conservative: Preserves tooth structure; no veneer/crown preparation needed.
├:: Cost-Effective: Lower cost than indirect restorations.
├:: Predictable: Gradual, controlled lightening; natural appearance.
└:: Patient Comfort: Minimal sensitivity; no in-office visits during bleaching phase.
```

**Disadvantages:**
```
├── Multiple Visits: Requires 2-4 appointments for optimal results.
├── Time: 3-7 days between applications; total treatment 2-4 weeks.
├:: Cervical Barrier Critical: Inadequate barrier risks external cervical resorption.
├:: Limited to Non-Vital Teeth: Not for vital tooth discoloration.
└:: Shade Limitations: May not achieve perfect match with adjacent teeth.
```

**Complications & Prevention:**

*External Cervical Resorption:*
```
├── Cause: Peroxide diffusion through dentinal tubules to periodontium.
├── Prevention: Always place 2mm cervical barrier; avoid in teeth with cracks/defects.
├:: Management: If occurs, stop bleaching; treat resorption surgically if possible.
└:: Incidence: <1% with proper barrier placement vs. 5-10% with high-concentration H₂O₂.
```

*Inadequate Lightening:*
```
├── Cause: Insufficient applications, severe discoloration.
├── Prevention: Realistic expectations; consider veneer/crown if bleaching fails.
├:: Management: Additional applications (max 4) or restorative alternative.
└:: Success rate: 80-90% for mild-moderate discoloration; lower for severe staining.
```

*Sensitivity:*
```
├── Cause: Barrier failure, excessive concentration.
├── Prevention: Proper barrier technique; use sodium perborate (not high-concentration H₂O₂).
├:: Management: Desensitizing agents; delay final restoration if needed.
└:: Incidence: Rare with proper technique; usually transient.
```

**Evidence-Based Outcomes:**
```
├── Success rate: 80-90% for mild-moderate discoloration with proper technique.
├── Long-term stability: 70-80% maintain shade at 5 years; touch-ups possible.
├:: Safety: Cervical resorption risk <1% with proper barrier placement.
├:: Patient satisfaction: High for conservative approach; natural results.
└:: Cost-effectiveness: Lower cost than veneers/crowns; good value for appropriate cases.
```

*Note: Walking bleach is first-line treatment for non-vital tooth discoloration; always prioritize barrier placement over bleaching speed. Sodium perborate is preferred over high-concentration hydrogen peroxide for safety.*

---

### 9. Etiology of tooth discolouration - 4m (479) 📝

**Classification by Origin:**

*I. Extrinsic Stains (Surface):*
```
├── Dietary:
│   ├── Coffee, tea, red wine, berries: Chromogens bind to pellicle.
│   ├── Tobacco: Tar/nicotine deposits; brown/black stains.
│   └:: Management: Professional prophylaxis; air-polishing; improved hygiene.
│
├── Medications:
│   ├── Chlorhexidine mouthrinse: Brown staining with prolonged use.
│   ├── Iron supplements: Black stains; liquid iron particularly staining.
│   ├── Tetracycline (topical): Rare; systemic tetracycline causes intrinsic staining.
│   └:: Management: Discontinue if possible; professional cleaning.
│
├── Poor Oral Hygiene:
│   ├── Plaque/calculus accumulation: Yellow/brown stains.
│   ├── Bacterial pigments: Black line stain; chromogenic bacteria.
│   └:: Management: Improved hygiene; professional cleaning; antimicrobial rinses.
│
├── Occupational:
│   ├── Metal dust exposure (iron, copper): Staining of enamel.
│   └:: Management: Protective equipment; professional cleaning.
│
└:: Management Principles:
    ├── Prevention: Good oral hygiene; limit staining foods/drinks.
    ├── Professional cleaning: Prophylaxis, air-polishing for removal.
    └:: Patient education: Dietary counseling; smoking cessation.
```

*II. Intrinsic Stains (Within Tooth Structure):*

*A. Developmental:*
```
├── Amelogenesis Imperfecta:
│   ├── Defective enamel formation; yellow-brown discoloration.
│   ├── Types: Hypoplastic, hypomaturation, hypocalcified.
│   └:: Management: Veneers, crowns; preventive care for sensitive teeth.
│
├── Dentinogenesis Imperfecta:
│   ├── Opalescent dentin; blue-gray/brown discoloration.
│   ├── Associated with osteogenesis imperfecta.
│   └:: Management: Full-coverage crowns; preventive care.
│
├── Fluorosis:
│   ├── Excess fluoride during enamel development; white/brown mottling.
│   ├── Severity: Mild (white spots) to severe (brown staining, pitting).
│   └:: Management: Microabrasion, bleaching, veneers for moderate-severe cases.
│
├── Tetracycline Staining:
│   ├── Antibiotic incorporation during tooth development; gray/brown bands.
│   ├── Timing: Critical period: Second trimester to age 8.
│   ├── Severity: Depends on dose, duration, timing of exposure.
│   └:: Management: Bleaching (limited success); veneers/crowns for severe cases.
│
└:: Other Developmental:
    ├── Enamel hypoplasia: Localized defects; discoloration.
    ├── Systemic illnesses: High fever, malnutrition during development.
    └:: Management: Restorative options based on severity.
```

*B. Acquired:*
```
├── Pulp Necrosis:
│   ├── Hemoglobin breakdown products (hemosiderin, bilirubin) diffuse into dentin.
│   ├── Gray, brown, or pink discoloration visible clinically.
│   └:: Management: Walking bleach for non-vital teeth; RCT if not already done.
│
├── Trauma:
│   ├── Pulp hemorrhage → similar to necrosis staining.
│   ├── Internal resorption: Pink hue from vascular granulation tissue.
│   └:: Management: Vital bleaching if vital; walking bleach if non-vital.
│
├── Aging:
│   ├── Secondary dentin deposition; yellowing due to thinner enamel.
│   ├── Wear facets; dentin exposure.
│   └:: Management: Bleaching; veneers for significant esthetic concerns.
│
├── Restorative Materials:
│   ├── Amalgam tattoo: Gray discoloration from amalgam particles in tissue.
│   ├── Metal posts: Gray diffusion through dentin.
│   ├── Sealer extrusion: Staining from endodontic materials.
│   └:: Management: Walking bleach; restorative coverage if bleaching fails.
│
└:: Iatrogenic:
    ├── Bleaching complications: Over-bleaching → translucent, gray appearance.
    ├── Improper restoration margins: Staining at restoration-tooth interface.
    └:: Management: Prevention via careful technique; restorative correction if occurs.
```

*III. Combined Stains:*
```
├── Extrinsic + Intrinsic: Common in long-standing discoloration.
├── Example: Tetracycline-stained tooth with surface coffee stains.
├:: Management: Address intrinsic component first (bleaching), then extrinsic (prophylaxis).
```

**Diagnostic Approach:**
```
├── History:
│   ├── Onset: Sudden vs. gradual; associated with trauma, medication, illness.
│   ├── Progression: Stable vs. worsening; response to previous treatments.
│   ├── Medical/dental history: Medications, systemic conditions, dental treatments.
│   └:: Patient concerns: Esthetic demands; functional complaints.
│
├── Clinical Examination:
│   ├── Visual: Shade assessment; pattern (generalized vs. localized).
│   ├── Vitality testing: Determine if pulp vital/necrotic.
│   ├── Transillumination: Differentiate intrinsic vs. extrinsic; detect cracks.
│   ├── Periodontal assessment: Rule out gingival causes of discoloration.
│   └:: Radiographic assessment: Periapical status; restorations; root development.
│
├── Differential Diagnosis:
│   ├── Rule out: Caries, restoration margins, cracks mimicking discoloration.
│   ├── Intrinsic vs. extrinsic: Scrub test; extrinsic stains remove with pumice.
│   └:: Vital vs. non-vital: Pulp testing critical for treatment planning.
```

**Management Principles:**
```
├── Extrinsic Stains:
│   ├── Professional prophylaxis: Scaling, polishing, air-polishing.
│   ├── Patient education: Oral hygiene instruction; dietary counseling.
│   └:: Maintenance: Regular professional cleaning; home care reinforcement.
│
├── Intrinsic Vital Teeth:
│   ├── Mild: Microabrasion (HCl + pumice) for superficial enamel stains.
│   ├── Moderate: Vital bleaching (in-office or night guard).
│   ├── Severe: Veneers or crowns after bleaching fails.
│   └:: Prevention: Address etiology; prevent further staining.
│
├── Intrinsic Non-Vital Teeth:
│   ├── Walking bleach (sodium perborate) first-line.
│   ├── If fails: Internal bleaching with higher-concentration peroxide (in-office).
│   ├── Restorative coverage: Composite crown or porcelain veneer if bleaching unsuccessful.
│   └:: Critical: Cervical barrier essential to prevent external cervical resorption.
│
├── Combined Stains:
│   ├── Address intrinsic component first; then extrinsic.
│   ├── Comprehensive approach: Bleaching + prophylaxis + patient education.
│   └:: Realistic expectations: Some stains may not be completely removable.
```

**Evidence-Based Considerations:**
```
├── Bleaching Efficacy:
│   ├── Vital teeth: 70-90% achieve 2-8 shade improvement.
│   ├── Non-vital teeth: 80-90% success with walking bleach for mild-moderate discoloration.
│   └:: Tetracycline: Limited success; often requires veneers/crowns for acceptable results.
│
├── Safety:
│   ├── Vital bleaching: No evidence of enamel damage with proper use.
│   ├── Walking bleach: <1% cervical resorption risk with proper barrier.
│   └:: Sensitivity: Most common side effect; usually transient.
│
├── Long-Term Stability:
│   ├── Bleaching results: Stable 1-3 years; maintenance touch-ups extend longevity.
│   ├── Restorative options: Veneers/crowns provide permanent solution but irreversible.
│   └:: Patient compliance: Critical for maintaining results with bleaching.
```

*Note: Accurate diagnosis of discoloration etiology is essential for appropriate treatment planning. A systematic approach considering stain type, tooth vitality, and patient factors ensures optimal esthetic outcomes.*

---

### 10. Smile design & dental parameters - 4m (467, 468) 📝

**Definition:** Comprehensive esthetic planning process that considers facial, dental, and gingival parameters to create harmonious, natural-looking smiles.

**Facial Parameters:**

*Midline:*
```
├── Facial Midline: Vertical line through glabella, philtrum, chin.
├── Dental Midline: Should coincide with facial midline within 2-3mm.
├:: Cant: Horizontal plane of incisal edges should be parallel to interpupillary line.
├:: Clinical Significance: Midline discrepancies >3mm noticeable; affect smile harmony.
└:: Management: Orthodontics, restorative correction, or acceptance if minor.
```

*Smile Arc:*
```
├── Definition: Curve of incisal edges relative to lower lip curvature during smile.
├── Ideal: Incisal edges follow curvature of lower lip (consonant smile arc).
├:: Types:
│   ├── Consonant: Ideal; incisal edges parallel to lower lip curve.
│   ├── Straight: Less esthetic; incisal edges form straight line.
│   └:: Reverse: Least esthetic; incisal edges curve opposite to lip.
│
├:: Clinical Significance: Consonant smile arc perceived as youthful, attractive.
└:: Management: Incisal edge position adjustment via restorations or orthodontics.
```

*Gingival Parameters:*

*Gingival Zenith:*
```
├── Definition: Most apical point of gingival margin for each tooth.
├── Ideal Position:
│   ├── Central incisors: Zenith slightly distal to long axis.
│   ├── Lateral incisors: Zenith slightly distal to long axis.
│   ├── Canines: Zenith slightly distal to long axis.
│   └:: Symmetry: Bilateral symmetry critical for esthetics.
│
├:: Clinical Significance: Asymmetry or incorrect zenith position disrupts smile harmony.
└:: Management: Gingival recontouring, crown lengthening, or restorative compensation.
```

*Gingival Display:*
```
├── Ideal: 1-3mm of gingival display during full smile.
├:: Excessive Display (>3mm): "Gummy smile"; may require orthognathic surgery, crown lengthening, or Botox.
├:: Insufficient Display (<1mm): May appear aged; consider crown lengthening or incisal edge lengthening.
└:: Clinical Assessment: Evaluate at rest, social smile, full smile.
```

*Dental Parameters:*

*Tooth Proportions:*
```
├── Width-to-Length Ratio:
│   ├── Ideal: 75-80% (width 75-80% of length).
│   ├── Central incisors: Typically 8-9mm wide, 10-11mm long.
│   ├── Lateral incisors: Slightly smaller; 6-7mm wide, 9-10mm long.
│   └:: Canines: Similar width to centrals; longer length.
│
├:: Golden Proportion:
    ├── Theoretical ratio: 1.618:1 (width of central to lateral to canine).
    ├── Clinical reality: Rarely achieved naturally; guide rather than rule.
    └:: Application: Use as reference; prioritize natural appearance over mathematical perfection.
```

*Axial Inclinations:*
```
├── Definition: Angulation of long axis of teeth relative to vertical.
├── Ideal:
│   ├── Central incisors: Slight distal inclination.
│   ├── Lateral incisors: More distal inclination than centrals.
│   ├── Canines: Similar to laterals or slightly more upright.
│   └:: Symmetry: Bilateral symmetry critical.
│
├:: Clinical Significance: Incorrect inclinations affect light reflection, smile dynamics.
└:: Management: Orthodontics, restorative contouring, or acceptance if minor.
```

*Embrasures:*
```
├── Definition: V-shaped spaces between adjacent teeth apical to contact area.
├── Types: Incisal, gingival, facial, lingual embrasures.
├:: Ideal: Progressive increase in size from anterior to posterior.
├:: Clinical Significance: Affect light transmission, food escape, gingival health.
└:: Management: Proper contact position, contouring during restoration.
```

*Surface Characteristics:*
```
├── Line Angles:
│   ├── Mesial/distal line angles: Define tooth width perception.
│   ├── Incisal line angles: Affect incisal edge appearance.
│   └:: Clinical Significance: Influence light reflection; affect perceived tooth shape.
│
├:: Texture:
    ├── Perikymata: Horizontal ridges on enamel; more prominent in younger teeth.
    ├── Lobes: Developmental features; mamelons on incisal edges.
    └:: Clinical Significance: Add natural character; avoid overly smooth, artificial appearance.
│
├:: Translucency:
    ├── Incisal third: More translucent; mimics natural enamel.
    ├── Body: More opaque; mimics dentin.
    └:: Clinical Significance: Critical for natural appearance; achieved through layering techniques.
```

*Color Parameters:*

*Value (Lightness):*
```
├── Definition: Perceived lightness/darkness of tooth.
├── Most important color parameter for esthetics.
├:: High value: Bright, youthful appearance.
├:: Low value: Dark, aged appearance.
└:: Clinical Significance: Value mismatch most noticeable color discrepancy.
```

*Chroma (Saturation):*
```
├── Definition: Intensity or purity of color.
├── High chroma: Vivid, saturated color.
├:: Low chroma: Pale, washed-out color.
└:: Clinical Significance: Affects perceived tooth vitality; natural teeth have moderate chroma.
```

*Hue (Color Family):*
```
├── Definition: Basic color (red, yellow, blue, etc.).
├── Natural teeth: Predominantly yellow-red hue.
├:: Clinical Significance: Less critical than value; subtle hue differences often acceptable.
└:: Management: Shade selection in natural light; multiple shade tabs.
```

**Smile Design Process:**

*Diagnostic Phase:*
```
├── Comprehensive Examination:
│   ├── Facial analysis: Midline, smile arc, lip dynamics.
│   ├── Dental analysis: Tooth proportions, inclinations, embrasures.
│   ├── Gingival analysis: Zenith position, display, symmetry.
│   └:: Color analysis: Value, chroma, hue; shade documentation.
│
├:: Diagnostic Records:
    ├── Photographs: Full face, smile, retracted views; natural and artificial light.
    ├── Radiographs: Periapical, bitewing for underlying structure.
    ├── Study models: For wax-up, diagnostic mock-up.
    └:: Digital planning: CAD software for virtual smile design.
```

*Planning Phase:*
```
├── Diagnostic Wax-up:
│   ├── Physical or digital wax-up of proposed changes.
│   ├── Allows visualization of proposed changes.
│   └:: Basis for provisional restorations, patient approval.
│
├:: Provisional Restorations:
    ├── Fabricated from wax-up; trial smile for patient.
    ├── Allows functional and esthetic evaluation.
    └:: Feedback incorporated into final design.
│
├:: Patient Communication:
    ├── Visual aids: Photos, wax-up, digital simulations.
    ├── Realistic expectations: Discuss limitations, alternatives.
    └:: Shared decision-making: Patient input on final design.
```

*Execution Phase:*
```
├── Minimally Invasive Approach:
│   ├── Preserve sound tooth structure whenever possible.
│   ├── Adhesive techniques for conservative preparations.
│   └:: Reversible options first (bleaching, bonding) before irreversible (crowns).
│
├:: Material Selection:
    ├── Composite: For minimal changes; repairable; cost-effective.
    ├── Porcelain veneers: For moderate changes; superior esthetics.
    ├── Full crowns: For extensive changes; maximum strength.
    └:: Material properties: Match to clinical demands and patient expectations.
│
├:: Technical Execution:
    ├── Precise preparation design: Respect biological width; preserve enamel.
    ├── Accurate impressions/scans: Critical for fit and esthetics.
    ├── Laboratory communication: Detailed instructions; shade photos; wax-up reference.
    └:: Try-in protocol: Verify fit, contacts, occlusion, esthetics before cementation.
```

*Evaluation Phase:*
```
├── Final Assessment:
│   ├── Esthetics: Shade match, contour, symmetry, natural appearance.
│   ├── Function: Occlusion, phonetics, comfort.
│   ├── Biology: Marginal integrity, gingival health, pulp vitality.
│   └:: Patient satisfaction: Address concerns; ensure expectations met.
│
├:: Maintenance Protocol:
    ├── Oral hygiene instruction: Special care for restorations.
    ├── Recall schedule: Regular monitoring of restoration integrity.
    └:: Long-term planning: Anticipate future needs; preserve options.
```

**Evidence-Based Considerations:**
```
├── Patient Satisfaction:
│   ├── 85-90% satisfaction when smile design principles followed.
│   ├── Key factors: Natural appearance, function, minimal invasiveness.
│   └:: Dissatisfaction often due to unrealistic expectations or poor communication.
│
├:: Longevity:
    ├── Veneers: 90-95% 10-year survival with proper case selection and technique.
    ├── Composite bonding: 5-7 year survival; repairable.
    └:: Full crowns: 90%+ 10-year survival; more invasive but durable.
│
├:: Minimally Invasive Priority:
    ├── Bleaching before veneers; bonding before crowns.
    ├── Preserve enamel for optimal bonding and longevity.
    └:: Reversible options preferred when esthetically acceptable.
```

*Note: Smile design is both art and science, requiring understanding of esthetic principles, technical skill, and patient-centered communication. Successful outcomes depend on comprehensive diagnosis, careful planning, precise execution, and realistic expectations.*

---

### 11. Dimensions of colour - 2m (459) 📝

**Definition:** Three attributes used to describe and match tooth color in restorative dentistry.

**Three Dimensions:**

*1. Value (Lightness):*
```
├── Definition: Perceived lightness or darkness of a color.
├── Most important dimension for esthetic matching.
├:: High value: Bright, light appearance (youthful).
├:: Low value: Dark, shadowed appearance (aged).
├:: Clinical Significance: Value mismatch most noticeable color discrepancy.
└:: Measurement: VITA Classical scale A1-D4; value increases from D4 (darkest) to A1 (lightest).
```

*2. Chroma (Saturation):*
```
├── Definition: Intensity or purity of a color; how vivid or dull.
├── High chroma: Vivid, saturated color (intense yellow/red).
├:: Low chroma: Pale, washed-out color (grayish).
├:: Clinical Significance: Affects perceived tooth vitality; natural teeth have moderate chroma.
└:: Measurement: Within each VITA shade group (A, B, C, D), chroma increases with number (A1 < A2 < A3 < A4).
```

*3. Hue (Color Family):*
```
├── Definition: Basic color family (red, yellow, blue, etc.).
├── Natural teeth: Predominantly yellow-red hue.
├:: Clinical Significance: Less critical than value; subtle hue differences often acceptable.
└:: Measurement: VITA shade groups: A (reddish-brown), B (reddish-yellow), C (gray), D (reddish-gray).
```

**Clinical Application:**
```
├── Shade Selection:
│   ├── Natural daylight or color-corrected lighting.
│   ├── Evaluate value first (most important), then chroma, then hue.
│   ├── Compare to adjacent teeth; consider value gradient from cervical to incisal.
│   └:: Take multiple shade tabs; document with photos.
│
├:: Shade Communication:
    ├── Use standardized systems: VITA Classical, VITA 3D-Master.
    ├── Include value, chroma, hue in laboratory instructions.
    ├── Provide shade photos in natural light.
    └:: Note special characteristics: Translucency, opalescence, characterization.
│
├:: Shade Matching Challenges:
    ├── Metamerism: Color appears different under different light sources.
    ├── Surface texture: Affects light reflection; polished vs. rough surfaces.
    └:: Underlying structure: Dentin color affects final restoration appearance.
```

*Note: Understanding the three dimensions of color is fundamental to successful esthetic restorations. Value is the most critical parameter for shade matching, followed by chroma and hue. Proper shade selection technique and communication with the laboratory are essential for predictable esthetic outcomes.*
